> DEVELOPER

can you do the below except use our OWN ocal app instead of installing from external? I want you to improve the quality of this codebase. To do this, install and run desloppify.
Run ALL of the following (requires Python 3.11+):

pip install --upgrade "desloppify[full]"
desloppify update-skill claude    # installs the full workflow guide — pick yours: claude, cursor, codex, copilot, windsurf, gemini

Before scanning, check for directories that should be excluded (vendor, build output,
generated code, worktrees, etc.) and exclude obvious ones with `desloppify exclude <path>`.
Share any questionable candidates with me before excluding.

desloppify scan --path .
desloppify next

--path is the directory to scan (use "." for the whole project, or "src/" etc).

Your goal is to get the strict score as high as possible. The scoring resists gaming — the
only way to improve it is to actually make the code better.

THE LOOP: run `next`. It is the execution queue from the living plan, not the whole backlog.
It tells you what to fix now, which file, and the resolve command to run when done.
Fix it, resolve it, run `next` again. Over and over. This is your main job.

Use `desloppify backlog` only when you need to inspect broader open work that is not currently
driving execution.

Don't be lazy. Large refactors and small detailed fixes — do both with equal energy. No task
is too big or too small. Fix things properly, not minimally.

Use `plan` / `plan queue` to reorder priorities or cluster related issues. Rescan periodically.
The scan output includes agent instructions — follow them, don't substitute your own analysis.

> DEVELOPER

DO they seem to justify the score?

> AGENT

Yes, broadly.

For the `76-88` range these findings look proportionate: they are real architectural and workflow quality issues, but not catastrophic design failure. The recurring pattern is not “broken code”; it is “too many live facades, wrappers, and contract splits,” especially around triage, review orchestration, and compatibility layers.

The lower scores look justified most clearly by:
- The real import cycle around language hook registration in [hook_registry.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/hook_registry.py) and the registry package.
- The still-live split between [state.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state.py), [state_compat.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state_compat.py), and [state_io.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/state_io.py).
- The observe-stage contract drift between prompt logic and CLI guidance in [rendering.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/stages/rendering.py), [observe.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/stages/observe.py), and [stage_prompts_observe.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/runner/stage_prompts_observe.py).
- Wrapper churn in [plan_reconcile.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/scan/plan_reconcile.py) and validation/review helper facades.

Why the scores should not be lower:
- Mechanical quality is already very high.
- The system does have intentional seams and strong workflow guardrails.
- Most findings are cleanup/refactor debt, not evidence of chaotic ownership or unsafe behavior.

So my read is:
- `dependency_health 88` and the low-80s scores seem fair.
- `cross_module_architecture 76` also seems fair because that cycle plus boundary duplication is a real architectural penalty.
- Nothing in the completed batches looks inflated or unserious.

Once the import finishes, I’d trust these scores as a reasonable baseline and then work the queue from the architectural/contract-drift items first.

> AGENT

Yes. The positive findings look mostly thoughtful, not like filler.

The strongest positives are specific and paired with an actual design reason:
- “Review packet pipeline has explicit contracts” points to packet hashing and contract checks across prepare, orchestration, and submit.
- “Frozen triage issue snapshot” identifies a concrete stability seam in the triage workflow.
- “Optional extras are feature-gated” is a real dependency-health observation, not generic praise.
- “Enum-backed zone contracts are consistent” and “Typed schemas exist for core state” are grounded in actual type surfaces.
- “Lookup versus enforcement split” in auth is especially good because it recognizes a subtle modeling choice, not just code cleanliness.

My read:
- The positives are generally evidence-based and useful.
- They seem materially more thoughtful than boilerplate praise.
- A few are somewhat broad, like “command helper prefixes are consistent,” but even those are still tied to a concrete naming pattern.

So overall: yes, the positives look credible, and they support the scores rather than weakening them.

> AGENT

Because I gave you two different kinds of numbers.

What I shared earlier for examples like `error_consistency: 79.0` came from the raw per-batch runner output while the review was still in flight, for example [batch-4.raw.txt](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/.desloppify/subagents/runs/20260312_182927/results/batch-4.raw.txt). `scorecard.png` and `desloppify status` use the final merged/imported scores from [holistic_issues_merged.json](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/.desloppify/subagents/runs/20260312_182927/holistic_issues_merged.json), not those raw batch numbers.

The important part is that the runner intentionally recalibrates scores during merge. That happens in [merge.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/review/batch/merge.py) via `_compute_merged_assessments(...)`, and the penalty logic is in [scoring.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/review/batch/scoring.py). It applies issue-pressure penalties and caps based on confirmed issues’ confidence, impact scope, and fix scope. So:
- Raw batch score: `error_consistency = 79.0`
- Final merged/imported score: `error_consistency = 71.0`
- Scorecard/status then shows `71.0%`

Same pattern explains the others:
- `authorization_consistency` raw `84.0` became final `79.4`
- `cross_module_architecture` raw `76.0` became final `59.8`

There is one more wrinkle:
- `Elegance` on the scorecard is a combined display of high/mid/low elegance.
- `Abstraction fit` is also shown as a combined calibrated score in the final payload.

So the mismatch is real, but it is not `scorecard.png` being wrong. The mistake was that I quoted interim raw batch scores instead of the finalized merged/imported scores that the scorecard is built from.

> DEVELOPER

Wait, the runner changes the scores? Why does that make sense?

> AGENT

Yes. The runner does not just average reviewer scores; it post-processes them.

Why that makes sense:
- A reviewer can give a dimension a broadly positive score and still report a few serious defects.
- If the tool took the raw score literally, you could end up with something like “`84` with three high-confidence subsystem issues,” which is internally inconsistent.
- The merge step forces the final score to reflect both:
  - the reviewer’s numeric judgment
  - the severity and count of the confirmed issues they reported

That logic is in [scoring.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/review/batch/scoring.py):
- it computes `issue_pressure` from each issue’s confidence, impact scope, and fix scope
- it applies a penalty to the raw score
- it can also cap the final score when a dimension has nontrivial open issues

So the intended model is:
- Raw batch score = “reviewer’s direct assessment”
- Final merged score = “assessment, made consistent with the actual defects imported”

Why that is defensible:
- It resists inflated scoring
- It makes the score track actionable debt, not just narrative tone
- It keeps “few issues + high score” and “many serious issues + high score” from looking equally healthy

The downside:
- It is easy to misread, because the tool exposes both numbers but mostly surfaces only the final one in status/scorecard.
- That makes the system feel opaque unless you know the merge policy.

My view: the idea is reasonable, but the transparency is weak. The tool should probably expose both explicitly:
- `assessment_score`
- `final_score_after_issue_pressure`

Right now it mostly hides that distinction, which is why your question came up.

> AGENT

Yes. There really are two behaviors, and that is why it skipped here.

On a fresh start, `next` uses the “initial review” path:
- `_is_fresh_boundary()` returns true when `plan_start_scores` is missing or reset in [snapshot.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py#L79).
- If there are unassessed subjective dimensions, `_phase_for_snapshot()` chooses `review_initial` first in [snapshot.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py#L143).
- Those “initial” dimensions are exactly the unassessed ones from `build_subjective_items()` in [synthetic.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/synthetic.py#L239).

After review import, the code switches to a different path:
- Review import appends the new `review::...` issue IDs to `plan["queue_order"]` in [reconcile_review_import.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_plan/reconcile_review_import.py#L140).
- It then injects triage, but triage injection explicitly appends stages to the back of the raw queue in [triage.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_plan/sync/triage.py#L59).
- `next` does not execute raw `queue_order` directly. It rebuilds a snapshot and uses `snapshot.execution_items` via [selection.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/selection.py#L25).
- In that snapshot, phase precedence is `review_postflight` before `triage_postflight` in [snapshot.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py#L159).
- And `postflight_review_items` is built as `subjective_postflight_items + review_issue_items` in [snapshot.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py#L239).

So in this case triage was not actually absent. It was injected, but it lost phase priority. The current plan file shows that too: the `triage::...` IDs are present, just pushed to the end of `queue_order`, while review items sit ahead of them in [plan.json](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/.desloppify/plan.json#L5).

So your intended invariant:
- `scan -> subjective review -> triage -> execute`

is not what the code currently guarantees.

What it currently guarantees is closer to:
- fresh start: `review_initial`
- after import: append review issues, append triage, then let `next` prefer `review_postflight` over triage

That is why it looked like triage got skipped. It was a real workflow-order mismatch, not just bad logging.

If you want, I can patch it so that once subjective review is imported, pending triage becomes the next execution phase before review-fix work is surfaced.

> DEVELOPER

remember the two scanerios: pre and post-flight, understand both, i tink post-lfight is working now

> AGENT

I’m treating them separately.

`Pre-flight` is the fresh-boundary path: no cycle in progress, unassessed subjective dimensions surface first, then once a review import produces real `review::...` findings those findings should feed triage before they become executable.

`Post-flight` is the drained-queue path: scan gate, any stale subjective reruns, workflow actions like `communicate-score`, then triage if the live review issue set is not yet triaged. I’m keeping that ordering intact and targeting the narrower bug: raw review findings should not leapfrog triage in either path.

> AGENT

I’m editing the queue snapshot logic now. The change is narrow: review findings stay in backlog, but they stop being eligible as execution items until triage is current for the live review issue set.

> AGENT

I’ve made the core queue gating change. Next I’m updating the lifecycle tests to prove the two-step behavior you care about: workflow/triage can still lead postflight, but raw review findings only execute after triage has actually run for them.

> AGENT

I changed the queue logic so raw `review::...` findings are no longer executable until triage is current for the live review issue set. The implementation is in [snapshot.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py): `next` still rebuilds from the snapshot, but now it uses triage state to decide whether review findings belong in `execution_items` or only in backlog.

That preserves the two scenarios separately:
- Fresh/pre-flight: initial subjective review items still come first. If that review produces open findings, those findings stay out of execution until triage has run for them.
- Post-flight: the existing boundary still works as `scan -> subjective reruns if needed -> workflow items -> triage`. I did not reorder that. I only stopped raw review findings from jumping ahead of triage.

I locked this in with lifecycle and snapshot tests in [test_lifecycle_transitions.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/tests/commands/test_lifecycle_transitions.py) and [test_sync_split_modules_direct.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/tests/engine/test_sync_split_modules_direct.py).

Verification passed:
- `python3 -m pytest -q desloppify/tests/commands/test_lifecycle_transitions.py desloppify/tests/engine/test_sync_split_modules_direct.py desloppify/tests/review/test_work_queue_plan_order_and_triage.py`
- `ruff check desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py desloppify/tests/engine/test_sync_split_modules_direct.py desloppify/tests/commands/test_lifecycle_transitions.py`

So the fix is narrow and intentional: it does not rewrite post-flight sequencing, it enforces the missing rule that review findings must go through triage before they become the thing `next` tells you to execute.

> AGENT

Right now, no: the live `next` queue is not showing triage. It shows a single optional below-target re-review item:
- `auto/under-target-review`
- action: `desloppify review --prepare --dimensions ...`

That is expected for the current state. The patch only changes behavior when there are open `review::...` findings that have not yet been triaged. In that case, those findings stay in backlog until triage is current.

For another reviewer, the shortest map is:

Start / pre-flight
- Initial subjective review detection: [build_subjective_items()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/synthetic.py#L179)
- Fresh-boundary check: [_is_fresh_boundary()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py#L81)
- Phase selection for initial review: [_phase_for_snapshot()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py#L164)
- Review import injects review IDs + triage: [sync_plan_after_review_import()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_plan/reconcile_review_import.py#L140)

End / post-flight
- Scan/workflow/triage partitioning: [_workflow_partitions()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py#L136)
- Postflight phase ordering: [_phase_for_snapshot()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py#L164)
- Lifecycle fallback/order model: [resolve_lifecycle_phase()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_plan/refresh_lifecycle.py#L96)
- Workflow items that lead postflight: [build_run_scan_item()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/synthetic_workflow.py#L246), [build_communicate_score_item()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/synthetic_workflow.py#L213), [build_create_plan_item()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/synthetic_workflow.py#L165)

The actual fix
- Gate raw review findings behind triage: [_executable_review_issue_items()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py#L105)
- Triage truth source used by that gate: [build_triage_snapshot()](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_plan/triage/snapshot.py#L188)

Net effect:
- Pre-flight: `initial review -> import findings -> triage -> execute findings`
- Post-flight: `scan -> subjective reruns if needed -> workflow -> triage -> execute findings`

The regression tests for this are in [test_lifecycle_transitions.py](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/tests/commands/test_lifecycle_transitions.py) and [test_sync_split_modules_direct.py](//Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/tests/engine/test_sync_split_modules_direct.py).

That makes the catch-all facade real, not hypothetical.\n  files_read: [desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/validation/core.py, desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/validation/__init__.py, desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/stages/organize.py]\n  recommendation: Keep the narrow validation modules as the canonical ownership boundary and migrate callers off `validation/core.py` over time. That is exactly the inconsistency the issue describes: corruption is converted into a fresh plan before higher-level queue/resolve code can distinguish degraded recovery from a legitimate empty plan.\n  files_read: [desloppify/engine/_plan/persistence.py]\n  recommendation: Unify plan loading around `PlanLoadStatus` or a similar explicit contract and stop letting `load_plan()` silently erase corruption for callers that need degraded-mode behavior.\n\n- hash: review::.::holistic::error_consistency::plugin_metadata_failure_isolation_varies\n  verdict: genuine\n  verdict_reasoning: The language registry discovery path isolates plugin failures and continues: `load_all()` catches import errors per plugin, stores them in `failures`, and only logs via `_report_load_errors_for_load_all()`. Those are two different failure policies for plugin-like metadata loading paths, so the issue's claim about inconsistent isolation is real.\n  files_read: [desloppify/languages/_framework/registry/discovery.py, desloppify/app/commands/move/language.py]\n  recommendation: Pick one policy for non-critical plugin metadata lookups. > AGENT

Yes. I found the exact failure path, and it is recoverable.

What happened:
- The run log shows `reflect` completed and was confirmed, then `organize` never launched. It failed in preflight with `reflect_ledger_mismatch(count=40 types=cluster,permanent_skip)` in [run.log](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/.desloppify/triage_runs/20260312_200204/run.log) and [run_summary.json](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/.desloppify/triage_runs/20260312_200204/run_summary.json).
- `organize` is configured as a `self_record` stage in [orchestrator_codex_pipeline_execution.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/runner/orchestrator_codex_pipeline_execution.py#L92). That means the subagent is supposed to create clusters and skips itself via CLI.
- The organize prompt explicitly tells it to do exactly that in [stage_prompts_instruction_blocks.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/runner/stage_prompts_instruction_blocks.py#L188).
- But before the subagent is allowed to run, `preflight_stage()` checks that the plan already matches the reflect ledger in [orchestrator_codex_pipeline_execution.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/runner/orchestrator_codex_pipeline_execution.py#L180).
- That check calls `validate_organize_against_reflect_ledger()` in [organize_policy.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/validation/organize_policy.py#L213), which expects the manual clusters and permanent skips to already exist in plan state.
- `reflect` does not create those clusters/skips. It only records the ledger and writes decisions into `epic_triage_meta.issue_dispositions` in [reflect.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/stages/reflect.py#L116).

So the bug is:
- `organize` is treated as “the stage that applies the blueprint”
- but its preflight requires “the blueprint has already been applied”

That is a logic deadlock, not operator error.

Why it failed on this run:
- Your current plan has the reflect dispositions stored, but no matching manual clusters and no permanent skips yet.
- So every reflect decision shows up as “unplaced,” and preflight rejects the stage before the subagent can do the work.

Is it recoverable?
- Yes.
- Nothing looks corrupted.
- `observe` and `reflect` both persisted successfully.
- The reflect blueprint is still in plan state, and the issue disposition map is intact.
- Recovery is straightforward: materialize the clusters/skips from the reflect blueprint, then continue with `organize`, `enrich`, `sense-check`, and completion.

My read:
- This is a real runner bug in the triage pipeline.
- It is separate from the earlier pre/post-flight queue-order issue.
- The missing guardrail is test coverage for the invariant: a `self_record` organize stage must be allowed to launch before its own ledger is applied.

If you want, I can patch this properly and then finish triage from the current saved state.

}

> AGENT

Root cause was a contract mismatch in the Codex triage runner.

`reflect` only records intent: it writes the coverage ledger and `issue_dispositions` into plan metadata in [reflect.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/stages/reflect.py#L101). `organize` is the stage that is supposed to materialize that intent into real plan mutations by creating manual clusters and permanent skips, and the runner explicitly treats it as `self_record` in [orchestrator_codex_pipeline_execution.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/runner/orchestrator_codex_pipeline_execution.py#L92). But `preflight_stage()` was validating the postcondition before launch by calling ledger reconciliation on the unmodified plan, which guaranteed `reflect_ledger_mismatch` for any fresh organize run. The organize prompt itself proves the intended flow: it tells the subagent to create clusters/skips via CLI during the stage in [stage_prompts_instruction_blocks.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/runner/stage_prompts_instruction_blocks.py#L188). So the bug was not in triage data; it was a stale runner guard checking “already applied” state for a stage whose job is “apply it now.”

I fixed that in [orchestrator_codex_pipeline_execution.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/runner/orchestrator_codex_pipeline_execution.py#L180) by making preflight aware of `prompt_mode`. `organize` in `self_record` mode now only checks upstream `reflect` validity before launch and leaves ledger/disposition reconciliation to the actual organize command, where it belongs. The wiring is passed through from `execute_stage()` in [orchestrator_codex_pipeline_execution.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/runner/orchestrator_codex_pipeline_execution.py#L659). I added regressions in [test_triage_split_modules_direct.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/tests/commands/plan/test_triage_split_modules_direct.py#L1102) for both cases: self-record organize is allowed before ledger materialization, while an output-only organize path still blocks on mismatch.

Verification passed:
- `ruff check` clean on the touched files
- `37 passed` in [test_triage_split_modules_direct.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/tests/commands/plan/test_triage_split_modules_direct.py)
- `22 passed` in [test_reflect_disposition_ledger.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/tests/commands/plan/test_reflect_disposition_ledger.py)

I also restarted the live triage run. It now gets past the old blocker and actually launches `organize` in the real workflow instead of dying immediately with `reflect_ledger_mismatch`. The triage run is not finished yet, but the specific runner deadlock is resolved.

> DEVELOPER

Look into them in more detail, the action_plans, what do you undersatnd from that?

> AGENT

From the saved `action_steps`, I read the plan as five different kinds of work, not one generic cleanup pass.

`review-state-canonicalization` in [plan.json](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/.desloppify/plan.json) is an internal-boundary migration. The action plans are saying:
- active command code should stop calling compatibility facades like `state_compat` and legacy importing helpers
- `dimension_character` should become the single internal judgment field
- import-time validation should reject incomplete review payloads instead of logging and continuing
- tests and batch models must be updated so the rename is real, not half-migrated

What I understand from that: this cluster is trying to remove dual internal representations. It is less “refactor for style” and more “stop carrying two live schemas and two state paths.”

`persistence-and-plugin-failure-contracts` is a behavioral contract cleanup. The steps mean:
- `load_plan()` should stop flattening corruption/recovery into “just an empty plan”
- queue/resolve code should consume explicit plan-load status instead of guessing degraded mode
- metadata-only plugin discovery should warn-and-continue consistently
- user-selected language operations can still hard-fail when the chosen target is actually required

What I understand from that: this cluster is about making failure modes explicit and consistent, especially for degraded plan recovery and non-critical plugin enumeration.

`triage-contract-and-snapshot-alignment` is narrower than the title sounds. The saved step details point to three real jobs:
- retype specific triage snapshot helpers to `PlanModel`/`Cluster`
- make observe confirmation use frozen triage state instead of recomputing from live state
- remove remaining `validation.core` umbrella imports, the filler completion text, and one legacy display shim

What I understand from that: this is not really “redesign triage.” It is “finish the contract cleanup around the triage snapshot and stop a few remaining soft/legacy seams from leaking into live command paths.” This is the cluster with the most refactor-churn risk, but the saved actions are still fairly concrete.

`framework-contract-hardening` is type-surface plus dependency-injection cleanup:
- replace anonymous dict payloads with named aliases/`TypedDict`s
- stop the hidden module-global swap in `phase_security`
- thread the typed aliases through the real framework contracts

What I understand from that: this cluster is trying to make framework/plugin seams explicit without changing the public phase result shape. It is low-level, but the intent is precise.

`legacy-surface-migration` splits into two very different actions:
- normalize two old detector facade modules to current style
- create a `treesitter/compat/` namespace and demote root underscore wrappers to compatibility shims
- then repoint tests/callers so only a small compatibility layer stays on the old imports

What I understand from that: the detector part is straightforward cleanup. The tree-sitter part is a package-taxonomy migration. That is the only action plan that still reads like meaningful churn even if executed correctly.

So the overall pattern I see is:
- three clusters are primarily contract tightening and internal migration
- one cluster is cleanup of triage seams that are still slightly fuzzy
- one cluster is a real namespace migration, which is the least obviously necessary part

If you want, I can go one level deeper and mark each individual action step as:
- `clear win`
- `worth doing but needs tight scope`
- `high churn / questionable`

> AGENT

Yes, it was a bug.

Why it happened:
- During `organize`, the runner legitimately used permanent skips on live review IDs; the log shows that explicitly in [organize.log](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/.desloppify/triage_runs/20260312_201302/logs/organize.log#L2584).
- `plan skip` clears the postflight scan marker for real issue IDs in [override_skip.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/override_skip.py#L248).
- Queue phase selection gives `scan` priority over postflight review work whenever that marker is missing in [snapshot.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_work_queue/snapshot.py#L164).
- Triaging finished, but completion never restored the marker, so `next` incorrectly surfaced `workflow::run-scan` instead of the freshly triaged queue.

So the mismatch was recoverable and real: triage had completed, but its own skip activity invalidated the lifecycle marker and completion failed to re-anchor the queue to the current scan.

I fixed it in [completion_flow.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/triage/completion_flow.py#L156) by restoring `postflight_scan_completed_at_scan_count` when triage completes against an existing scan. I added regressions in [test_triage_completion_cleanup.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/tests/commands/plan/test_triage_completion_cleanup.py#L221) to prove:
- completed triage stays on `review_postflight` instead of bouncing to `workflow::run-scan`
- no fake marker is written when there is no real scan history

Verification passed:
- `44 passed` across the focused lifecycle/triage tests
- `ruff check` clean on touched files

On your “should sense-check have caught this?” question: not really as a human-stage issue. The missing guard was not triage content review; it was a lifecycle invariant in completion. The current detail was also not adequate, because `next` only said “Run post-flight scan...” and did not explain that triage-time skip mutations had cleared the marker. The real missing piece was completion-path validation/test coverage, not another manual stage.

Current state: recovered. `desloppify next` now shows the actual queued work again, starting with `review-state-canonicalization`, instead of forcing a scan.

so would this definitely have stopped sscan from showing again?  and what's next in the queue?

> AGENT

I’m checking the live queue once more so I can answer with the current top item, not the last in-memory snapshot.

> AGENT

For this failure mode, yes.

What was broken was: triage finished on the current scan, but the postflight-scan marker had been cleared by triage-time skip mutations and never restored. The patch fixes exactly that, so in the same scenario `scan` should not jump back in front of the freshly triaged queue again.

Two legitimate cases can still make `scan` appear:
- there is no real scan history to anchor completion to
- later real issue mutations clear the marker again, meaning a new postflight scan is actually warranted

Right now the live queue is back to the intended execution order. Next is:
- `review-state-canonicalization`

After that:
- `persistence-and-plugin-failure-contracts`
- `triage-contract-and-snapshot-alignment`

If you want, I’ll start on `review-state-canonicalization` now.

I’m tracing every caller of `clear_postflight_scan_completion()` so I can give you the full list from code, not an approximation.

> AGENT

Yes. Current callers are:

- Resolving real issues in [living_plan.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/resolve/living_plan.py#L98)
- `plan skip` in [override_skip.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/override_skip.py#L248)
- `plan unskip` in [override_skip.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/override_skip.py#L307)
- `plan backlog` in [override_skip.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/override_skip.py#L377)
- `plan reopen` in [override_misc.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/app/commands/plan/override_misc.py#L116)

Important nuance: it only actually clears for real queue issues. Synthetic IDs like `workflow::...`, `triage::...`, or `subjective::...` are ignored by the guard in [refresh_lifecycle.py](/Users/user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/engine/_plan/refresh_lifecycle.py#L150).

So the risky pattern is any command that mutates live issue coverage after a postflight scan. The one that bit us was `plan skip --permanent` during triage `organize`.

> DEVELOPER

so no other ones that could be negative?

> AGENT

Nothing else obvious in the current codebase.

The only direct clearers are those five call sites, and all of them are defensible in principle because they mutate real issue coverage:
- resolve
- skip
- unskip
- backlog
- reopen

The negative case was specific:
- `skip` during triage is valid
- but triage completion should have restored the “same scan, now execute” state afterward
- that restoration was missing

So my read is:
- `no`, I do not see another equally wrong clearer right now
- `yes`, any future workflow that mutates real issues during a staged process could hit the same class of bug if its completion path forgets to re-establish the lifecycle marker

The general risk is not “too many clearers,” it is “completion paths need to repair the marker when they intentionally mutate coverage without requiring a fresh scan.”
